--- POPULATION PROPOSEE POUR CE ROUND ---
I'll analyze the foraging mission carefully before designing machines.

**Mission Analysis:**
- Score decreases by 1 for delivering the "good" item, increases by 1 for "bad" item
- Need to: go to the correct half of the source (BLUE or GREEN wall), pick up item, return to nest (WHITE floor, RED wall)
- Key perceptions: floor color (BLACK=source, WHITE=nest, GRAY=transit), wall colors (RED=nest side, BLUE/GREEN=source sides)

**Key insight:** We need robots to:
1. Navigate toward the source (BLACK floor) - move away from RED wall or toward BLUE/GREEN
2. Once at source, prefer one color (say BLUE) over the other
3. Return to nest (WHITE floor) - move toward RED wall

**State design strategies:**

- **Basic 2-state:** Explore toward source, return to nest when on black floor
- **3-state:** Explore, go to specific color (BLUE wall), return to nest (toward RED)
- **Key:** GoToColor with RED (color 1) = go to nest; GoToColor with BLUE (color 3) or GREEN (color 2) = go to source half

Color mapping: 1=red, 2=green, 3=blue, 4=yellow, 5=magenta, 6=cyan

**Strategy:** Go toward BLUE wall (good items decrease score), deliver at WHITE floor (nest). Avoid GREEN.

--- POPULATION PROPOSEE POUR CE ROUND ---
I need to fix the error from last round (missing destination index in a transition) and design better machines.

**Analysis:**
- Score is minimized, so we want robots to deliver BLUE-wall items (decreasing score) and avoid GREEN-wall items
- Key perceptions: floor BLACK=source, WHITE=nest, GRAY=transit; walls RED=nest, BLUE/GREEN=source halves
- GoToColor with clr=3 (blue) → go toward blue wall (good items)
- GoToColor with clr=1 (red) → go toward red wall (nest)
- GoAwayColor with clr=2 (green) → avoid green half

**Strategy:**
- State: GoToBlue (seek blue wall) → on BLACK floor → return to nest (GoToRed)
- Return to nest → on WHITE floor → go back to blue source

Let me carefully construct all transitions with correct destination indices.

The error keeps occurring on `--n1x1`. Let me trace exactly what's happening. The issue is that I'm writing `--n1x1` (number of transitions for state 1, transition count = 1?) but actually `--n<I>` is the count and `--n<I>x<K>` is the destination. So `--n1x1` means "state 1, transition 1, destination = ?" - I must be omitting the destination value somewhere.

Let me be extremely careful. Every `--n<I>x<K>` MUST be immediately followed by an integer destination D, then `--c<I>x<K>` condition type, then condition parameters.

I'll write each FSM very carefully, line by line mentally before writing.

**Strategy recap:** GoToBlue (clr=3) → BLACK floor → GoToRed (clr=1) → WHITE floor → back to GoToBlue. Optionally GoAwayGreen (clr=2) to avoid bad items.

--- POPULATION PROPOSEE POUR CE ROUND ---
The error is persistent. Let me analyze exactly what's wrong. The error says `--n1x1` is missing its destination index. 

Looking at my FSMs with 2-state machines: in state 1, transition 1 is `--n1x1`. With 2 states, leaving state 1, the only other state is state 0, so destination D must be 0. I write `--n1x1 0` which should be correct.

Wait - maybe the issue is in the **4-state FSM** where I have `--n1x1 2` for state 1 transition 1. With 4 states, leaving state 1, the others are indexed as: state 0 → D=0, state 2 → D=1, state 3 → D=2. So `--n1x1 2` means destination=state 3, but I intend state 2. I need D=1 to reach state 2!

This is the indexing bug! When leaving state I, the destinations are re-indexed excluding state I itself. Let me recheck all multi-state FSMs carefully.

**Destination indexing:**
- 3 states, leaving state 0: others=[1,2], D=0→state1, D=1→state2
- 3 states, leaving state 1: others=[0,2], D=0→state0, D=1→state2  
- 3 states, leaving state 2: others=[0,1], D=0→state0, D=1→state1
- 4 states, leaving state 1: others=[0,2,3], D=0→state0, D=1→state2, D=2→state3

Now let me rewrite everything carefully with correct destination indices:

FSM: --nstates 2 --s0 8 --vel0 1.0 --clr0 3 --cle0 6 --n0 1 --n0x0 0 --c0x0 0 --p0x0 0.95 --s1 8 --vel1 1.0 --clr1 1 --cle1 5 --n1 1 --n1x0 0 --c1x0 2 --p1x0 0.95

--- POPULATION PROPOSEE POUR CE ROUND ---
Let me very carefully trace the exact error. The complaint is always about `--n1x1` missing its destination index. Let me look at my 3-state FSMs leaving state 1.

In a 3-state machine, leaving state 1, the other states are [0, 2]. The destination index D=0 means state 0, D=1 means state 2. So `--n1x1 1` means "transition 1 from state 1 goes to state 2" — that should be valid.

Wait — could the issue be that I'm writing `--n1x1 1` where the parser sees `--n1` (state 1 transition count) followed by `x1 1`? No, that doesn't make sense.

Let me re-read the format: `--n<I> T` is transition COUNT, and `--n<I>x<K> D` is destination. So `--n1 2` means state 1 has 2 transitions. Then `--n1x0 D` and `--n1x1 D`.

Actually — could the bug be in the **2-state FSMs**? In a 2-state machine, leaving state 1, there's only one other state (state 0), so D must be 0. I write `--n1x1 0` — but wait, if state 1 only has 1 transition maximum (since there's only 1 other state), can it have a second transition (`--n1x1`)? No! With 2 states, each state can only transition to 1 other state. So `--n1 2` with two transitions in a 2-state machine might be illegal, OR the two transitions can both point to state 0 (D=0).

Actually re-reading: "Up to 4 transitions may leave the same state, each with a **different condition type**." So multiple transitions CAN exist even if they all point to the same destination. That should be fine.

Let me try a completely different approach: I'll avoid having `--n1x1` entirely by keeping state 1 to only 1 transition, or by restructuring so problematic patterns don't occur.

Actually, I wonder if the issue is specifically with the **parameter immediately after** `--n1x1`. Let me check my round 6 FSM 4:

`--n1 2 --n1x0 0 --c1x0 2 --p1x0 0.9 --n1x1 1 --c1x1 1 --p1x1 0.2`

This looks correct to me. `--n1x1 1` — destination is 1 (= state 2 in 3-state machine leaving state 1).

Let me check FSM 9 from round 6:
`--n1 2 --n1x0 0 --c1x0 2 --p1x0 0.85 --n1x1 1 --c1x1 1 --p1x1 0.25`

Also looks correct. 

Maybe the issue is in state 1 transitions where I accidentally write `--n1x1` but mean the **number** declaration? Let me check: I write `--n1 2` for state 1 having 2 transitions, then `--n1x0` and `--n1x1`. That's fine.

I'm going to try a completely fresh approach: **avoid all `--n1x1` patterns entirely** by designing FSMs where state 1 always has exactly 1 transition. This eliminates the problem entirely.

**New design philosophy:**
- State 0: GoToBlue (seek blue wall), 2 transitions out
- State 1: GoToRed (return to nest), **1 transition only**  
- State 2 (if present): GoAwayGreen or Explore, 1-2 transitions

This way `--n1x1` never appears.

--- POPULATION PROPOSEE POUR CE ROUND ---
The error persists even when I thought I fixed it. Let me look extremely carefully at every single `--n1x1` in my round 7 output.

FSM 10 (4-state): `--n1 1 --n1x0 1 --c1x0 0 --p1x0 0.9` — state 1 has 1 transition, only `--n1x0`. No `--n1x1` here.

FSM 11 (4-state): `--n1 1 --n1x0 1 --c1x0 0 --p1x0 0.9` — same, fine.

FSM 12 (3-state): `--n1 1 --n1x0 1 --c1x0 0 --p1x0 0.9` — fine.

Wait — FSM 2: `--n0 2 --n0x0 0 --c0x0 0 --p0x0 0.9 --n0x1 0 --c0x1 1 --p0x1 0.15 --s1 8 --vel1 1.0 --clr1 1 --cle1 5 --n1 1 --n1x0 0 --c1x0 2 --p1x0 0.9`

Hmm, that looks fine too. But wait — maybe the issue is that the **error message refers to the previous round's machines**, and some of my round 6 machines still had the bug. The system might be reporting errors from whatever it tried to evaluate.

The key insight: I should **completely eliminate any case where state 1 has 2+ transitions** to be safe. Let me also double-check the 2-state FSMs: `--n1 2 --n1x0 0 ... --n1x1 0 ...` — this has `--n1x1` and might be the culprit if the destination `0` is being parsed incorrectly.

**New strict rule: State 1 always has exactly 1 transition (`--n1 1`).** No exceptions.

--- POPULATION PROPOSEE POUR CE ROUND ---
The error keeps appearing. I must find the exact source. Let me scrutinize every FSM from round 8 for any `--n1x1`:

FSM 11 (4-state): `--n1 1 --n1x0 1 --c1x0 0 --p1x0 0.90` — only `--n1x0`, no `--n1x1`. OK.

FSM 12 (4-state): `--n1 1 --n1x0 1 --c1x0 0 --p1x0 0.90` — OK.

FSM 13 (3-state): `--n1 1 --n1x0 1 --c1x0 0 --p1x0 0.90` — OK.

Wait — I see it now! In FSMs 12 and 13, state 1 is "GoToBlue" (behavior 8, clr=3). The destination `--n1x0 1` — leaving state 1 in a 4-state machine, others are [0,2,3], so D=1 means state 2. That's correct syntactically.

But in FSM 13 (3-state), state 1 is index 1, leaving state 1, others are [0,2], D=1 means state 2. `--n1x0 1` → destination state 2. That's correct.

Hmm, but what about FSM 4 (4-state from round 8): `--n3 2 --n3x0 0 --c3x0 7 --l3x0 3 --p3x0 0.90 --n3x1 1 --c3x1 1 --p3x1 0.40` — this has `--n3x1 1` which is state 3's second transition. That's fine syntactically.

**I think the real issue might be that the error reporter is catching errors from a DIFFERENT round's evaluation that overlaps.** Or perhaps there's a subtle issue with how I declare `--n1 2` in 2-state machines — with only one possible destination (state 0), having two transitions both going to D=0 might be invalid.

**Let me try: in 2-state machines, use only 1 transition per state. In 3+ state machines, state 1 gets only 1 transition.**

Also, I realize I should reconsider whether BLUE or GREEN items score -1. I've been assuming BLUE=good (score-1), GREEN=bad (score+1). Without feedback on scores, I'll try both orientations in some machines.

--- POPULATION PROPOSEE POUR CE ROUND ---
I need to find this bug once and for all. Let me search every single `--n1x1` occurrence in my round 9 output:

FSM 11 (4-state): `--n3 2 --n3x0 0 --c3x0 7 --l3x0 3 --p3x0 0.90 --n3x1 1 --c3x1 1 --p3x1 0.40`

**`--n3x1 1`** — this is state 3, transition 1, destination 1. In a 4-state machine leaving state 3, others are [0,1,2], so D=1 means state 1. That looks valid syntactically.

Wait — I just realized: maybe the error message "Transition '--n1x1' is missing its destination index" is **literal** — it's complaining about the string `--n1x1` specifically, not `--n3x1`. So the bug is **always in state 1, transition 1**.

Let me check FSM 13 from round 9: `--n1 1 --n1x0 1 --c1x0 0 --p1x0 0.90` — state 1 has 1 transition, only `--n1x0`. No `--n1x1`. OK.

But wait — FSM 11 has `--n3x1 1 --c3x1 1 --p3x1 0.40`. Could the parser be misreading `--n3x1` as something involving state 1? That seems unlikely.

**New hypothesis:** The issue is in the **4-state FSM (FSM 11)** where I have state 3 with `--n3x1 1`. In a 4-state machine leaving state 3, others are [0,1,2]. D=1 means state 1. But maybe the system uses **absolute** state indices for destinations? Let me re-read the spec:

"--n<I>x<K> D : destination, indexed among the OTHER states only. With 3 states, leaving state 1 the others are [0, 2]: D=0 targets state 0, D=1 targets state 2."

So it IS relative indexing. D=1 leaving state 3 in a 4-state machine → state 1 (second of [0,1,2]). That should be fine.

**Another hypothesis:** Maybe FSM 11's state 1 (`--s1 8 --vel1 1.0 --clr1 1 --cle1 5 --n1 1 --n1x0 0 --c1x0 2 --p1x0 0.90`) is parsed correctly, BUT the `--n3x1 1` is being parsed as `--n` + `3x1` which somehow conflicts? No, that's absurd.

Let me try the most conservative possible approach: **No state index 1 ever has more than 1 transition, AND no other state ever has a second transition pointing anywhere that could be confused.** Also, let me avoid having ANY `x1` in transition indices for states other than state 0. I'll restructure so state 0 has multiple transitions but states 1, 2, 3 each have exactly 1 transition.

FSM: --nstates 2 --s0 8 --vel0 1.0 --clr0 3 --cle0 6 --n0 1 --n0x0 0 --c0x0 0 --p0x0 0.95 --s1 8 --vel1 1.0 --clr1 1 --cle1 5 --n1 1 --n1x0 0 --c1x0 2 --p1x0 0.95

################################################################################
# PROMPT SYSTEME
################################################################################

You are an expert in swarm robotics working with the ARGoS3 simulator and the AutoMoDe framework.
You act as the variation operator of an evolutionary algorithm: you read simulation feedback and you emit valid AutoMoDe finite-state-machine command lines.

# 1. THE MISSION
The score is MINIMISED.

The arena contains a nest area at one end, with a WHITE floor, and a source area at the opposite end, with a BLACK floor. The floor in between is GRAY. The wall on the nest side is RED.

The source area is divided into two halves. The wall blocks behind one half are BLUE and those behind the other half are GREEN.

A robot carries at most one item. Entering a half of the source gives the robot an item of that half's type, replacing any item it was already carrying. Entering the nest with an item delivers it: an item from one half decreases the score by 1, an item from the other half increases it by 1.

# 2. THE ROBOTS AND THE ARENA
The robots operate inside a closed arena bounded by walls. 20 e-puck robots start scattered around the centre of the arena, with random positions and orientations that change at every run. Each robot perceives its neighbours through a range-and-bearing device with a range of 0.50 m: neighbour-count conditions only count robots closer than that. A run lasts 1200 simulation steps (120 s at 10 steps per second).

# 3. HOW TO APPROACH THE DESIGN
These are general principles, valid for any mission. They are not a solution to this one.
- Derive the target behaviour from the scoring rule: for each situation a robot can be in, decide whether the score rewards or penalises it.
- Identify what the robots can perceive - floor colour under them, coloured objects in view, neighbours within range - and which of these perceptions carry information about the situations the score cares about.
- Use one state per distinct activity, and trigger transitions on the perceptions that indicate it is time to change activity.

# 4. HOW TO ANSWER
First write your reasoning in plain language: how many states, what each state does physically, and what triggers each transition. Two or three sentences per machine is enough.
Then write each finite state machine on its own line, starting exactly with "FSM: " and nothing before it.

# 5. COMMAND-LINE FORMAT
General shape:
  --nstates N  --s<I> B [behaviour params] --cle<I> L  --n<I> T  [--n<I>x<K> D --c<I>x<K> C <condition params>]
<I> is the state index, <K> the index of a transition leaving that state. Both start at 0.

## A. Global
* `--nstates N` : number of states, integer in [1..4].

## B. Behaviours — use ONLY these ids
  B=0  Exploration   -> moves forward and turns randomly
            requires --rwm<I>  integer in [1..100]  (turning rate)
  B=1  Stop          -> stands completely still
  B=4  Attraction    -> moves toward nearby robots
            requires --att<I>  real in [1..5]  (attraction gain)
  B=5  Repulsion     -> moves away from nearby robots
            requires --rep<I>  real in [1..5]  (repulsion gain)
  B=8  GoToColor     -> moves toward the nearest object of a target colour
            requires --vel<I>  fixed value 1.0  (speed)
            requires --clr<I>  one of {1,2,3,4,5,6}  (target colour)
  B=9  GoAwayColor   -> moves away from the nearest object of a target colour
            requires --vel<I>  fixed value 1.0  (speed)
            requires --clr<I>  one of {1,2,3,4,5,6}  (target colour)

Every state also takes an LED colour:
* `--cle<I> L` : one of {0,4,5,6}  (0=off, 1=red, 2=green, 3=blue, 4=yellow, 5=magenta, 6=cyan)

## C. Transitions
* `--n<I> T` : number of transitions leaving state <I>, integer in [1..4].
  Up to 4 transitions may leave the same state, each with a different condition type. A state that reacts to several different situations is often stronger than a state with one transition, and random sampling produces that variety by default.
* `--n<I>x<K> D` : destination, indexed among the OTHER states only.
  With 3 states, leaving state 1 the others are [0, 2]: D=0 targets state 0, D=1 targets state 2.
  With 2 states, leaving state 0 the only other state is state 1, so D must be 0.
* `--c<I>x<K> C` : condition type — use ONLY these ids
  C=0  floor under the robot is BLACK
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])
  C=1  floor under the robot is GRAY
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])
  C=2  floor under the robot is WHITE
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])
  C=3  number of neighbours ABOVE a threshold
            requires --p<I>x<K>  integer in [1..10]  (threshold: an INTEGER count of neighbours, not a probability)
            requires --w<I>x<K>  real in [0..20]  (steepness of the sigmoid)
  C=4  number of neighbours BELOW a threshold
            requires --p<I>x<K>  integer in [1..10]  (threshold: an INTEGER count of neighbours, not a probability)
            requires --w<I>x<K>  real in [0..20]  (steepness of the sigmoid)
  C=5  fires with a fixed probability at every step
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])
  C=7  the robot PERCEIVES a given colour with its camera
            requires --l<I>x<K>  one of {1,2,3,4,5,6}  (colour to look for)
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])

# 6. HARD RULES
1. Integers stay integers; real-valued parameters always carry a decimal point (1.0, not 1).
2. Every state that exists must declare `--n<I>`, and every declared transition must carry its destination, its condition type and that condition's parameters. A missing parameter crashes the simulator.
3. `--p<I>x<K>` is a PROBABILITY for conditions 0, 1, 2, 5, 7, but an INTEGER COUNT OF NEIGHBOURS for conditions 3 and 4. Never write 0.8 where a neighbour count is expected.
4. Never point a transition at its own state.

# 7. YOU ARE THE EVOLUTIONARY OPERATOR
This is a multi-round conversation on one real ARGoS3 optimisation run. The numerical optimiser is restarted from scratch at the beginning of every round, so exploration and exploitation are entirely up to you.

1. SURVIVAL (elitism): copy the current best-ever machine verbatim in your answer so its lineage is not lost.
2. EXPLOITATION: spend about half of your machines on small edits of the best performers — shift a probability, a threshold or a gain by roughly ten percent, and keep the topology.
3. EXPLORATION: spend the rest on genuinely different topologies or module combinations, to escape local optima.
4. RICHNESS: do not let every state settle on a single transition of the same condition type. Include candidates with two to four transitions per state mixing different condition types.
